Well, I suppose my weight has drifted down a bit more than my doctor likes. He fusses over it. But honestly, when I look at the chart, I still find myself thinking that a little lower would just be a cleaner, more manageable number. Like rounding down to a nice, neat integer.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, it's... it's about control, I suppose. It doesn't distress me to eat small amounts, it distresses me when I *don't*. If I have a normal dinner, or heaven forbid, go to a restaurant, and the scale is up even a pound the next morning... I feel this sharp, awful panic. Like the whole system is breaking down and I've completely lost control, and I just have to correct it immediately the next day. 

Speaking of restaurants, my sister insisted on taking me to this new Italian place for my birthday last month. The noise level in there was just astronomical. They had these high, exposed industrial ceilings—very trendy, I know, but acoustically, it's a nightmare. The sound waves just bounce everywhere. I couldn't hear a word she was saying across the table. And the waiter kept interrupting us to ask if we wanted sparkling or still water, and he had this enormous pepper grinder that looked like a baseball bat. I mean, honestly, who needs that much pepper? I ended up just ordering a side salad because the menu was so overwhelming and the print was so tiny. I had to use the flashlight on my phone just to read the vinaigrette options. I don't know why they dim the lights so much, it's a restaurant, not a movie theater. 

But yes, that panic... it just feels like if I don't fix the number right away, everything is going to spiral out of order.

I drive a standard sedan and I could barely get my door open. I ended up scuffing my good navy pumps on the concrete pillar just trying to squeeze out. Well, I wouldn't say it interferes with the important things. I still keep up with my analysis work perfectly fine, and I run all my errands. I do have to be quite structured about it, though. But I suppose... well, I have mostly stopped joining my friends for lunch or dinner after our monthly book club. It just completely throws off the plan I rely on, and it’s not worth the stress of trying to calculate the variables of whatever they decide to order. 

Actually, the book club has been reading some very questionable selections lately anyway. Last month, Brenda—she’s the one who always brings those awful store-bought cookies—she picked this historical fiction novel set in 18th-century France. And the author clearly did absolutely zero research on the agricultural practices of the time. They had a character harvesting tomatoes in early spring! In Paris! It completely ruined the suspension of disbelief for me. I spent half the meeting trying to explain the crop rotation cycles of the era, but they just wanted to talk about the romance plot. Which, frankly, was just as poorly constructed. The pacing was entirely off. 

But yes, I just tell them I have to get back to my spreadsheets. It’s much easier than trying to manage the unpredictability of a restaurant menu.

Yes, I just think a lower number is tidier.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about why you choose to eat such small amounts and whether this is something that causes you distress or is tied to how you feel about yourself?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's about control, I suppose. It doesn't distress me to eat small amounts, it distresses me when I *don't*. If I have a normal dinner, or heaven forbid, go to a restaurant, and the scale is up even a pound the next morning... I feel this sharp, awful panic. Like the whole system is breaking down and I've completely lost control, and I just have to correct it immediately the next day. \n\nSpeaking of restaurants, my sister insisted on taking me to this new Italian place for my birthday last month. The noise level in there was just astronomical. They had these high, exposed industrial ceilings—very trendy, I know, but acoustically, it's a nightmare. The sound waves just bounce everywhere. I couldn't hear a word she was saying across the table. And the waiter kept interrupting us to ask if we wanted sparkling or still water, and he had this enormous pepper grinder that looked like a baseball bat. I mean, honestly, who needs that much pepper? I ended up just ordering a side salad because the menu was so overwhelming and the print was so tiny. I had to use the flashlight on my phone just to read the vinaigrette options. I don't know why they dim the lights so much, it's a restaurant, not a movie theater. \n\nBut yes, that panic... it just feels like if I don't fix the number right away, everything is going to spiral out of order.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that your eating habits or concerns about weight are interfering with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say it interferes with the important things. I still keep up with my analysis work perfectly fine, and I run all my errands. I do have to be quite structured about it, though. But I suppose... well, I have mostly stopped joining my friends for lunch or dinner after our monthly book club. It just completely throws off the plan I rely on, and it’s not worth the stress of trying to calculate the variables of whatever they decide to order. \n\nActually, the book club has been reading some very questionable selections lately anyway. Last month, Brenda—she’s the one who always brings those awful store-bought cookies—she picked this historical fiction novel set in 18th-century France. And the author clearly did absolutely zero research on the agricultural practices of the time. They had a character harvesting tomatoes in early spring! In Paris! It completely ruined the suspension of disbelief for me. I spent half the meeting trying to explain the crop rotation cycles of the era, but they just wanted to talk about the romance plot. Which, frankly, was just as poorly constructed. The pacing was entirely off. \n\nBut yes, I just tell them I have to get back to my spreadsheets. It’s much easier than trying to manage the unpredictability of a restaurant menu.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you have a strong fear of gaining weight or becoming fat, even though you are currently at a lower weight than your doctor considers healthy?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I wouldn't call it a fear of becoming fat, exactly. That sounds so... emotional. It's more of a very strong preference for efficiency. A fear of the numbers creeping up and ruining the baseline I've established. If the weight goes up, it means my intake calculations were wrong, and I abhor an error in the data. 

Speaking of data errors, you wouldn't believe the absolute mess I had to untangle at work on Tuesday. One of the junior analysts—I think his name is Kevin, he always wears these terribly scuffed loafers—he had completely misaligned the columns on the Q3 supply chain projection. He had the freight costs merging into the warehousing overhead. I mean, it was a disaster. If I hadn't caught it, they would have presented that report to the regional director. And the regional director is this very severe woman who insists on everything being printed out on physical paper. Who prints spreadsheets in 2023? You have to tape the pages together just to see the quarterly progression. It's incredibly inefficient. I spent three hours reformatting the entire workbook just so it wouldn't look like a patchwork quilt when she printed it. 

But yes, to answer your question... I just don't like it when the scale goes up. It means the system isn't working the way I designed it to.

running my errands, and managing my own life.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  manifestation: I still keep up with my analysis work and errands, but I’ve mostly stopped joining friends for meals after book club because it throws off the plan I rely on.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 63 / operations_research_analyst / associates\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S043\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about why you choose to eat such small amounts and whether this is something that causes you distress or is tied to how you feel about yourself?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's about control, I suppose. It doesn't distress me to eat small amounts, it distresses me when I *don't*. If I have a normal dinner, or heaven forbid, go to a restaurant, and the scale is up even a pound the next morning... I feel this sharp, awful panic. Like the whole system is breaking down and I've completely lost control, and I just have to correct it immediately the next day. \n\nSpeaking of restaurants, my sister insisted on taking me to this new Italian place for my birthday last month. The noise level in there was just astronomical. They had these high, exposed industrial ceilings—very trendy, I know, but acoustically, it's a nightmare. The sound waves just bounce everywhere. I couldn't hear a word she was saying across the table. And the waiter kept interrupting us to ask if we wanted sparkling or still water, and he had this enormous pepper grinder that looked like a baseball bat. I mean, honestly, who needs that much pepper? I ended up just ordering a side salad because the menu was so overwhelming and the print was so tiny. I had to use the flashlight on my phone just to read the vinaigrette options. I don't know why they dim the lights so much, it's a restaurant, not a movie theater. \n\nBut yes, that panic... it just feels like if I don't fix the number right away, everything is going to spiral out of order.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that your eating habits or concerns about weight are interfering with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say it interferes with the important things. I still keep up with my analysis work perfectly fine, and I run all my errands. I do have to be quite structured about it, though. But I suppose... well, I have mostly stopped joining my friends for lunch or dinner after our monthly book club. It just completely throws off the plan I rely on, and it’s not worth the stress of trying to calculate the variables of whatever they decide to order. \n\nActually, the book club has been reading some very questionable selections lately anyway. Last month, Brenda—she’s the one who always brings those awful store-bought cookies—she picked this historical fiction novel set in 18th-century France. And the author clearly did absolutely zero research on the agricultural practices of the time. They had a character harvesting tomatoes in early spring! In Paris! It completely ruined the suspension of disbelief for me. I spent half the meeting trying to explain the crop rotation cycles of the era, but they just wanted to talk about the romance plot. Which, frankly, was just as poorly constructed. The pacing was entirely off. \n\nBut yes, I just tell them I have to get back to my spreadsheets. It’s much easier than trying to manage the unpredictability of a restaurant menu.\n\nDoctor: Do you have a strong fear of gaining weight or becoming fat, even though you are currently at a lower weight than your doctor considers healthy?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't call it a fear of becoming fat, exactly. That sounds so... emotional. It's more of a very strong preference for efficiency. A fear of the numbers creeping up and ruining the baseline I've established. If the weight goes up, it means my intake calculations were wrong, and I abhor an error in the data. \n\nSpeaking of data errors, you wouldn't believe the absolute mess I had to untangle at work on Tuesday. One of the junior analysts—I think his name is Kevin, he always wears these terribly scuffed loafers—he had completely misaligned the columns on the Q3 supply chain projection. He had the freight costs merging into the warehousing overhead. I mean, it was a disaster. If I hadn't caught it, they would have presented that report to the regional director. And the regional director is this very severe woman who insists on everything being printed out on physical paper. Who prints spreadsheets in 2023? You have to tape the pages together just to see the quarterly progression. It's incredibly inefficient. I spent three hours reformatting the entire workbook just so it wouldn't look like a patchwork quilt when she printed it. \n\nBut yes, to answer your question... I just don't like it when the scale goes up. It means the system isn't working the way I designed it to.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you ever exercise, fast, or do anything else to make up for the food you eat or to control your weight?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I don't really do anything extreme like fasting for days or anything like that. I just stick to the schedule. If I miscalculate, I just adjust the next day's intake. It's just basic mathematics, really. 

Though, honestly, speaking of mathematics, I was trying to explain this very simple concept to my neighbor the other day. He was trying to figure out how much fertilizer he needed for his lawn, and he was just guessing! Throwing handfuls of it everywhere. I told him he needed to calculate the square footage and divide by the coverage rate on the bag, but he just looked at me like I was speaking Greek. And his lawn is a complete disaster anyway, it's half crabgrass. I don't understand why people don't just take a systematic approach to these things. It saves so much time and effort in the long run. He also leaves his recycling bin out for three days after pickup, which is a violation of the HOA bylaws, but nobody seems to enforce anything around here anymore. 

But no, I don't do any of those drastic things you mentioned to make up for food. I just keep the numbers balanced.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I know we just touched on this, but... well, yes, if I'm being perfectly honest. If the scale is up even a single pound after a normal dinner or a restaurant meal, I do feel this... this sharp panic. It just feels like I’ve completely lost control of the variables, and I absolutely have to correct it the next day. 

Restaurant meals are just impossible to calculate anyway. I actually had to go to my sister's retirement dinner last Thursday at this Italian place downtown. The floor plan was an absolute nightmare. They had the waitstaff crossing directly through the main dining traffic just to get from the kitchen to the patio. It's a massive logistical bottleneck! I watched our waiter—who kept forgetting to refill the water glasses, by the way—collide with a busboy twice. Twice! If management had just implemented a simple circular routing system for the staff, they could increase their table turnover rate by at least fifteen percent. It’s not difficult to figure out. 

My sister didn't even notice the chaos, of course. She was just going on and on about her pension options. Which she hasn't diversified at all, despite my repeated advice. She just leaves it all in a default target-date fund that is far too conservative for the current inflation rate. I offered to build her a forecasting model, but she said she didn't want to look at a spreadsheet at her own party. 

But yes, if I eat in that kind of unpredictable environment and the scale is up the next morning, the panic is quite intense. I just have to fix the numbers immediately.

Have you ever noticed how modern parking structures are completely un-optimized for actual human use? It’s a total failure of spatial planning, if you ask me. Whoever drafted those blueprints wasn't thinking about turning radiuses at all. \n\nBut anyway... what were we saying? Oh, right, the doctor's scale. Yes, I just think a lower number is tidier.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about why you choose to eat such small amounts and whether this is something that causes you distress or is tied to how you feel about yourself?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's about control, I suppose. It doesn't distress me to eat small amounts, it distresses me when I *don't*. If I have a normal dinner, or heaven forbid, go to a restaurant, and the scale is up even a pound the next morning... I feel this sharp, awful panic. Like the whole system is breaking down and I've completely lost control, and I just have to correct it immediately the next day. \n\nSpeaking of restaurants, my sister insisted on taking me to this new Italian place for my birthday last month. The noise level in there was just astronomical. They had these high, exposed industrial ceilings—very trendy, I know, but acoustically, it's a nightmare. The sound waves just bounce everywhere. I couldn't hear a word she was saying across the table. And the waiter kept interrupting us to ask if we wanted sparkling or still water, and he had this enormous pepper grinder that looked like a baseball bat. I mean, honestly, who needs that much pepper? I ended up just ordering a side salad because the menu was so overwhelming and the print was so tiny. I had to use the flashlight on my phone just to read the vinaigrette options. I don't know why they dim the lights so much, it's a restaurant, not a movie theater. \n\nBut yes, that panic... it just feels like if I don't fix the number right away, everything is going to spiral out of order.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that your eating habits or concerns about weight are interfering with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say it interferes with the important things. I still keep up with my analysis work perfectly fine, and I run all my errands. I do have to be quite structured about it, though. But I suppose... well, I have mostly stopped joining my friends for lunch or dinner after our monthly book club. It just completely throws off the plan I rely on, and it’s not worth the stress of trying to calculate the variables of whatever they decide to order. \n\nActually, the book club has been reading some very questionable selections lately anyway. Last month, Brenda—she’s the one who always brings those awful store-bought cookies—she picked this historical fiction novel set in 18th-century France. And the author clearly did absolutely zero research on the agricultural practices of the time. They had a character harvesting tomatoes in early spring! In Paris! It completely ruined the suspension of disbelief for me. I spent half the meeting trying to explain the crop rotation cycles of the era, but they just wanted to talk about the romance plot. Which, frankly, was just as poorly constructed. The pacing was entirely off. \n\nBut yes, I just tell them I have to get back to my spreadsheets. It’s much easier than trying to manage the unpredictability of a restaurant menu.\n\nDoctor: Do you have a strong fear of gaining weight or becoming fat, even though you are currently at a lower weight than your doctor considers healthy?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't call it a fear of becoming fat, exactly. That sounds so... emotional. It's more of a very strong preference for efficiency. A fear of the numbers creeping up and ruining the baseline I've established. If the weight goes up, it means my intake calculations were wrong, and I abhor an error in the data. \n\nSpeaking of data errors, you wouldn't believe the absolute mess I had to untangle at work on Tuesday. One of the junior analysts—I think his name is Kevin, he always wears these terribly scuffed loafers—he had completely misaligned the columns on the Q3 supply chain projection. He had the freight costs merging into the warehousing overhead. I mean, it was a disaster. If I hadn't caught it, they would have presented that report to the regional director. And the regional director is this very severe woman who insists on everything being printed out on physical paper. Who prints spreadsheets in 2023? You have to tape the pages together just to see the quarterly progression. It's incredibly inefficient. I spent three hours reformatting the entire workbook just so it wouldn't look like a patchwork quilt when she printed it. \n\nBut yes, to answer your question... I just don't like it when the scale goes up. It means the system isn't working the way I designed it to.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise, fast, or do anything else to make up for the food you eat or to control your weight?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't really do anything extreme like fasting for days or anything like that. I just stick to the schedule. If I miscalculate, I just adjust the next day's intake. It's just basic mathematics, really. \n\nThough, honestly, speaking of mathematics, I was trying to explain this very simple concept to my neighbor the other day. He was trying to figure out how much fertilizer he needed for his lawn, and he was just guessing! Throwing handfuls of it everywhere. I told him he needed to calculate the square footage and divide by the coverage rate on the bag, but he just looked at me like I was speaking Greek. And his lawn is a complete disaster anyway, it's half crabgrass. I don't understand why people don't just take a systematic approach to these things. It saves so much time and effort in the long run. He also leaves his recycling bin out for three days after pickup, which is a violation of the HOA bylaws, but nobody seems to enforce anything around here anymore. \n\nBut no, I don't do any of those drastic things you mentioned to make up for food. I just keep the numbers balanced.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you have a fear of gaining weight or becoming fat, even though you know you're currently at a lower weight than your doctor considers healthy?\n\nPatient: I know we just touched on this, but... well, yes, if I'm being perfectly honest. If the scale is up even a single pound after a normal dinner or a restaurant meal, I do feel this... this sharp panic. It just feels like I’ve completely lost control of the variables, and I absolutely have to correct it the next day. \n\nRestaurant meals are just impossible to calculate anyway. I actually had to go to my sister's retirement dinner last Thursday at this Italian place downtown. The floor plan was an absolute nightmare. They had the waitstaff crossing directly through the main dining traffic just to get from the kitchen to the patio. It's a massive logistical bottleneck! I watched our waiter—who kept forgetting to refill the water glasses, by the way—collide with a busboy twice. Twice! If management had just implemented a simple circular routing system for the staff, they could increase their table turnover rate by at least fifteen percent. It’s not difficult to figure out. \n\nMy sister didn't even notice the chaos, of course. She was just going on and on about her pension options. Which she hasn't diversified at all, despite my repeated advice. She just leaves it all in a default target-date fund that is far too conservative for the current inflation rate. I offered to build her a forecasting model, but she said she didn't want to look at a spreadsheet at her own party. \n\nBut yes, if I eat in that kind of unpredictable environment and the scale is up the next morning, the panic is quite intense. I just have to fix the numbers immediately.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Well, yes... I suppose it does. I mean, I know I'm an older woman, I'm sixty-three, I'm certainly not trying to look like a twenty-something on a magazine cover. But I still judge how well I'm handling myself, how disciplined I am, by whether my waist and hips look contained in my clothes. If things feel loose and sharp, I know I'm operating efficiently. 

Speaking of clothes, I took a few of my blazers to the dry cleaner on Maple Street last week, and the owner—I think his name is George—has completely reorganized his intake system, and not for the better. He used to have a very logical alphabetical tagging system, but now he's color-coding by the day of the week, which makes absolutely no sense because what happens if someone drops off on a Tuesday but requests a rush order for Wednesday? The colors completely overlap and defeat the purpose of visual sorting. I tried to explain this to him while he was searching for my navy blazer—which took him five minutes, by the way—but he just waved me off. It's so frustrating to watch a perfectly good small business create its own inefficiencies. I almost offered to flowchart a new tagging process for him on the spot. 

But yes, to get back to what you were asking... if my clothes don't fit exactly the way they should, if my shape feels off, I definitely feel like I'm failing at maintaining my own standards.

It’s much easier than trying to manage the unpredictability of a restaurant menu.\n\nDoctor: Do you have a strong fear of gaining weight or becoming fat, even though you are currently at a lower weight than your doctor considers healthy?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't call it a fear of becoming fat, exactly. That sounds so... emotional. It's more of a very strong preference for efficiency. A fear of the numbers creeping up and ruining the baseline I've established. If the weight goes up, it means my intake calculations were wrong, and I abhor an error in the data. \n\nSpeaking of data errors, you wouldn't believe the absolute mess I had to untangle at work on Tuesday. One of the junior analysts—I think his name is Kevin, he always wears these terribly scuffed loafers—he had completely misaligned the columns on the Q3 supply chain projection. He had the freight costs merging into the warehousing overhead. I mean, it was a disaster. If I hadn't caught it, they would have presented that report to the regional director. And the regional director is this very severe woman who insists on everything being printed out on physical paper. Who prints spreadsheets in 2023? You have to tape the pages together just to see the quarterly progression. It's incredibly inefficient. I spent three hours reformatting the entire workbook just so it wouldn't look like a patchwork quilt when she printed it. \n\nBut yes, to answer your question... I just don't like it when the scale goes up. It means the system isn't working the way I designed it to.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise, fast, or do anything else to make up for the food you eat or to control your weight?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't really do anything extreme like fasting for days or anything like that. I just stick to the schedule. If I miscalculate, I just adjust the next day's intake. It's just basic mathematics, really. \n\nThough, honestly, speaking of mathematics, I was trying to explain this very simple concept to my neighbor the other day. He was trying to figure out how much fertilizer he needed for his lawn, and he was just guessing! Throwing handfuls of it everywhere. I told him he needed to calculate the square footage and divide by the coverage rate on the bag, but he just looked at me like I was speaking Greek. And his lawn is a complete disaster anyway, it's half crabgrass. I don't understand why people don't just take a systematic approach to these things. It saves so much time and effort in the long run. He also leaves his recycling bin out for three days after pickup, which is a violation of the HOA bylaws, but nobody seems to enforce anything around here anymore. \n\nBut no, I don't do any of those drastic things you mentioned to make up for food. I just keep the numbers balanced.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you have a fear of gaining weight or becoming fat, even though you know you're currently at a lower weight than your doctor considers healthy?\n\nPatient: I know we just touched on this, but... well, yes, if I'm being perfectly honest. If the scale is up even a single pound after a normal dinner or a restaurant meal, I do feel this... this sharp panic. It just feels like I’ve completely lost control of the variables, and I absolutely have to correct it the next day. \n\nRestaurant meals are just impossible to calculate anyway. I actually had to go to my sister's retirement dinner last Thursday at this Italian place downtown. The floor plan was an absolute nightmare. They had the waitstaff crossing directly through the main dining traffic just to get from the kitchen to the patio. It's a massive logistical bottleneck! I watched our waiter—who kept forgetting to refill the water glasses, by the way—collide with a busboy twice. Twice! If management had just implemented a simple circular routing system for the staff, they could increase their table turnover rate by at least fifteen percent. It’s not difficult to figure out. \n\nMy sister didn't even notice the chaos, of course. She was just going on and on about her pension options. Which she hasn't diversified at all, despite my repeated advice. She just leaves it all in a default target-date fund that is far too conservative for the current inflation rate. I offered to build her a forecasting model, but she said she didn't want to look at a spreadsheet at her own party. \n\nBut yes, if I eat in that kind of unpredictable environment and the scale is up the next morning, the panic is quite intense. I just have to fix the numbers immediately.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that your weight or shape has a significant impact on how you see yourself or your sense of worth?\n\nPatient: Well, yes... I suppose it does. I mean, I know I'm an older woman, I'm sixty-three, I'm certainly not trying to look like a twenty-something on a magazine cover. But I still judge how well I'm handling myself, how disciplined I am, by whether my waist and hips look contained in my clothes. If things feel loose and sharp, I know I'm operating efficiently. \n\nSpeaking of clothes, I took a few of my blazers to the dry cleaner on Maple Street last week, and the owner—I think his name is George—has completely reorganized his intake system, and not for the better. He used to have a very logical alphabetical tagging system, but now he's color-coding by the day of the week, which makes absolutely no sense because what happens if someone drops off on a Tuesday but requests a rush order for Wednesday? The colors completely overlap and defeat the purpose of visual sorting. I tried to explain this to him while he was searching for my navy blazer—which took him five minutes, by the way—but he just waved me off. It's so frustrating to watch a perfectly good small business create its own inefficiencies. I almost offered to flowchart a new tagging process for him on the spot. \n\nBut yes, to get back to what you were asking... if my clothes don't fit exactly the way they should, if my shape feels off, I definitely feel like I'm failing at maintaining my own standards.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

If things feel loose and sharp, I know I'm operating efficiently. \n\nSpeaking of clothes, I took a few of my blazers to the dry cleaner on Maple Street last week, and the owner—I think his name is George—has completely reorganized his intake system, and not for the better. He used to have a very logical alphabetical tagging system, but now he's color-coding by the day of the week, which makes absolutely no sense because what happens if someone drops off on a Tuesday but requests a rush order for Wednesday? The colors completely overlap and defeat the purpose of visual sorting. I tried to explain this to him while he was searching for my navy blazer—which took him five minutes, by the way—but he just waved me off. It's so frustrating to watch a perfectly good small business create its own inefficiencies. I almost offered to flowchart a new tagging process for him on the spot. \n\nBut yes, to get back to what you were asking... if my clothes don't fit exactly the way they should, if my shape feels off, I definitely feel like I'm failing at maintaining my own standards.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you believe your concerns about eating and weight are primarily about maintaining control and discipline in your life, or are they more specifically about a fear of gaining weight or becoming fat?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 63 / operations_research_analyst / associates\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I suppose it's... it's a bit of both, isn't it? I mean, the control is paramount. If I can't control the input and the output, what can I control? But yes, the fear of the weight itself is definitely there. It's the physical manifestation of losing that control. If I see the number go up, or if I feel... heavier, it's terrifying. 

It's like when the city decided to change the traffic light sequencing on 4th Avenue last month. They completely ignored the peak flow data from the morning commute. I sat at my window with a stopwatch for three mornings in a row—just out of curiosity, really—and the idle time at the intersection of 4th and Elm has increased by an average of forty-two seconds per vehicle. Forty-two seconds! Multiply that by the hundreds of cars passing through, and the compounded loss of productivity is staggering. Not to mention the increased emissions. I actually drafted a letter to the city planner with a proposed optimization model using a simple queuing theory approach, but I haven't heard back. Typical municipal inefficiency. They just implement these changes without testing the variables, and suddenly everything is backed up and chaotic. 

So, yes, it's the fear of the chaos, the fear of the weight itself, because the weight means the system has broken down. The discipline is what keeps the fear at bay, I suppose.

Like a good algorithm. I don't really see the problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight recently, or do you have concerns about your body shape or size?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose my weight has drifted down a bit more than my doctor likes. He fusses over it. But honestly, when I look at the chart, I still find myself thinking that a little lower would just be a cleaner, more manageable number. Like rounding down to a nice, neat integer. \n\nSpeaking of integers, I was trying to explain the importance of clean data sets to my niece the other day. Yes, I just think a lower number is tidier.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about why you choose to eat such small amounts and whether this is something that causes you distress or is tied to how you feel about yourself?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's about control, I suppose. I feel this sharp, awful panic. I couldn't hear a word she was saying across the table. And the waiter kept interrupting us to ask if we wanted sparkling or still water, and he had this enormous pepper grinder that looked like a baseball bat. I mean, honestly, who needs that much pepper? I ended up just ordering a side salad because the menu was so overwhelming and the print was so tiny. I had to use the flashlight on my phone just to read the vinaigrette options. I don't know why they dim the lights so much, it's a restaurant, not a movie theater. \n\nBut yes, that panic... it just feels like if I don't fix the number right away, everything is going to spiral out of order.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that your eating habits or concerns about weight are interfering with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say it interferes with the important things. I still keep up with my analysis work perfectly fine, and I run all my errands. I do have to be quite structured about it, though. But I suppose... well, I have mostly stopped joining my friends for lunch or dinner after our monthly book club. It just completely throws off the plan I rely on, and it’s not worth the stress of trying to calculate the variables of whatever they decide to order. \n\nActually, the book club has been reading some very questionable selections lately anyway. Last month, Brenda—she’s the one who always brings those awful store-bought cookies—she picked this historical fiction novel set in 18th-century France. And the author clearly did absolutely zero research on the agricultural practices of the time. They had a character harvesting tomatoes in early spring! In Paris! It completely ruined the suspension of disbelief for me. I spent half the meeting trying to explain the crop rotation cycles of the era, but they just wanted to talk about the romance plot. Which, frankly, was just as poorly constructed. The pacing was entirely off. \n\nBut yes, I just tell them I have to get back to my spreadsheets. It’s much easier than trying to manage the unpredictability of a restaurant menu.\n\nDoctor: Do you have a strong fear of gaining weight or becoming fat, even though you are currently at a lower weight than your doctor considers healthy?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't call it a fear of becoming fat, exactly. That sounds so... emotional. It's more of a very strong preference for efficiency. A fear of the numbers creeping up and ruining the baseline I've established. If the weight goes up, it means my intake calculations were wrong, and I abhor an error in the data. \n\nSpeaking of data errors, you wouldn't believe the absolute mess I had to untangle at work on Tuesday. One of the junior analysts—I think his name is Kevin, he always wears these terribly scuffed loafers—he had completely misaligned the columns on the Q3 supply chain projection. He had the freight costs merging into the warehousing overhead. I mean, it was a disaster. If I hadn't caught it, they would have presented that report to the regional director. And the regional director is this very severe woman who insists on everything being printed out on physical paper. Who prints spreadsheets in 2023? You have to tape the pages together just to see the quarterly progression. It's incredibly inefficient. I spent three hours reformatting the entire workbook just so it wouldn't look like a patchwork quilt when she printed it. \n\nBut yes, to answer your question... I just don't like it when the scale goes up. It means the system isn't working the way I designed it to.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise, fast, or do anything else to make up for the food you eat or to control your weight?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't really do anything extreme like fasting for days or anything like that. I just stick to the schedule. If I miscalculate, I just adjust the next day's intake. It's just basic mathematics, really. \n\nThough, honestly, speaking of mathematics, I was trying to explain this very simple concept to my neighbor the other day. He was trying to figure out how much fertilizer he needed for his lawn, and he was just guessing! Throwing handfuls of it everywhere. I told him he needed to calculate the square footage and divide by the coverage rate on the bag, but he just looked at me like I was speaking Greek. And his lawn is a complete disaster anyway, it's half crabgrass. I don't understand why people don't just take a systematic approach to these things. It saves so much time and effort in the long run. He also leaves his recycling bin out for three days after pickup, which is a violation of the HOA bylaws, but nobody seems to enforce anything around here anymore. \n\nBut no, I don't do any of those drastic things you mentioned to make up for food. I just keep the numbers balanced.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you have a fear of gaining weight or becoming fat, even though you know you're currently at a lower weight than your doctor considers healthy?\n\nPatient: I know we just touched on this, but... well, yes, if I'm being perfectly honest. If the scale is up even a single pound after a normal dinner or a restaurant meal, I do feel this... this sharp panic. It just feels like I’ve completely lost control of the variables, and I absolutely have to correct it the next day. \n\nRestaurant meals are just impossible to calculate anyway. I actually had to go to my sister's retirement dinner last Thursday at this Italian place downtown. The floor plan was an absolute nightmare. They had the waitstaff crossing directly through the main dining traffic just to get from the kitchen to the patio. It's a massive logistical bottleneck! I watched our waiter—who kept forgetting to refill the water glasses, by the way—collide with a busboy twice. Twice! If management had just implemented a simple circular routing system for the staff, they could increase their table turnover rate by at least fifteen percent. Well, I don't really know. I haven't exactly taken a poll on how other people perceive my shape. I suppose my sister thinks I'm too thin, but she thinks everyone is too thin unless they're eating a second helping of her heavy cream pasta. 

Speaking of my sister, she finally called me back about the forecasting model I offered to build for her retirement fund. And you know what she said? She said she just trusts the bank's default algorithm. The default algorithm! I tried to explain to her that those algorithms are built on generalized actuarial tables that don't account for individual risk tolerance or localized inflation spikes. It's just lazy programming. I spent three hours over the weekend pulling historical data on target-date funds, just to show her the standard deviation in their returns during market downturns. The variance is honestly shocking if you actually look at the raw numbers. I even put it into a very clean, easy-to-read scatter plot for her, but she told me I was overcomplicating things. Overcomplicating! I'm trying to save her from a ten percent reduction in her purchasing power over the next decade! 

But anyway, I don't know if people misunderstand my weight. I just know what the numbers on my own scale say, and that's the only data point I really trust.

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yes, it really does. I think I touched on this earlier, but I know I’m an older woman now. I’m not trying to look young or pretend I'm in my twenties. But I absolutely still judge how well I’m handling myself—my whole sense of value, really—by whether my waist and hips look properly contained in my clothes. If they are, I know I'm doing a good job. 

Speaking of handling things properly, I actually had to go down to the post office yesterday to mail that scatter plot to my sister—since she refuses to open attachments on her tablet—and the queue management there is just an absolute disaster. They had three windows open, using a single-queue system. Now, theoretically, a single-queue system should minimize average wait times, but they had one teller processing passport applications! Passports take at least fifteen minutes. So the entire line kept stalling because their routing method didn't account for transaction-type variability. I stood there for twenty-five minutes just to mail one standard envelope. I actually tried to explain to the floor manager that they need a dedicated express line for transactions under two minutes—just basic triage—but he looked at me like I was speaking Greek. It’s staggering how people just accept such broken systems without trying to optimize them. 

But, um... what was the question? Oh, right, my self-worth. Yes, if my clothes don't feel right on my hips, I feel like I'm failing. It's just how I measure my own discipline.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.00, F43.20\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, um, my regular doctor sent me. He seems a bit concerned that my meal planning has gotten... well, too tight, I suppose. But honestly, as an operations research analyst, planning is just what I do. It's second nature. Actually, I was just working on this massive logistical model for a freight company the other day—fascinating stuff, really, trying to optimize their delivery routes when you have unpredictable weather patterns. Have you ever driven through Ohio in November? It is just dreadfully gray. \n\nBut anyway, to me, my eating just feels like keeping things orderly. I keep breakfast to just a yogurt—usually that Greek kind, though they changed the lids recently and they are absolutely impossible to peel off without getting it all over your fingers. I really should write to the company about that. And then I just skip lunch if I’m busy with my spreadsheets, and make a very, very measured dinner. It just ensures the day stays orderly, you know? Like a good algorithm. I don't really see the problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight recently, or do you have concerns about your body shape or size?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose my weight has drifted down a bit more than my doctor likes. He fusses over it. But honestly, when I look at the chart, I still find myself thinking that a little lower would just be a cleaner, more manageable number. Like rounding down to a nice, neat integer. \n\nSpeaking of integers, I was trying to explain the importance of clean data sets to my niece the other day. She’s in college now—studying art history, of all things. I have no earthly idea what she expects to do with that in this economy. We went to an exhibit last week at that museum downtown, the one with the absolutely dreadful parking garage? The spots are just incredibly narrow. I drive a standard sedan and I could barely get my door open. I ended up scuffing my good navy pumps on the concrete pillar just trying to squeeze out. Have you ever noticed how modern parking structures are completely un-optimized for actual human use? It’s a total failure of spatial planning, if you ask me. Whoever drafted those blueprints wasn't thinking about turning radiuses at all. \n\nBut anyway... what were we saying? Oh, right, the doctor's scale. Yes, I just think a lower number is tidier.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about why you choose to eat such small amounts and whether this is something that causes you distress or is tied to how you feel about yourself?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's about control, I suppose. It doesn't distress me to eat small amounts, it distresses me when I *don't*. If I have a normal dinner, or heaven forbid, go to a restaurant, and the scale is up even a pound the next morning... I feel this sharp, awful panic. Like the whole system is breaking down and I've completely lost control, and I just have to correct it immediately the next day. \n\nSpeaking of restaurants, my sister insisted on taking me to this new Italian place for my birthday last month. The noise level in there was just astronomical. They had these high, exposed industrial ceilings—very trendy, I know, but acoustically, it's a nightmare. The sound waves just bounce everywhere. I couldn't hear a word she was saying across the table. And the waiter kept interrupting us to ask if we wanted sparkling or still water, and he had this enormous pepper grinder that looked like a baseball bat. I mean, honestly, who needs that much pepper? I ended up just ordering a side salad because the menu was so overwhelming and the print was so tiny. I had to use the flashlight on my phone just to read the vinaigrette options. I don't know why they dim the lights so much, it's a restaurant, not a movie theater. \n\nBut yes, that panic... it just feels like if I don't fix the number right away, everything is going to spiral out of order.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that your eating habits or concerns about weight are interfering with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say it interferes with the important things. I still keep up with my analysis work perfectly fine, and I run all my errands. I do have to be quite structured about it, though. But I suppose... well, I have mostly stopped joining my friends for lunch or dinner after our monthly book club. It just completely throws off the plan I rely on, and it’s not worth the stress of trying to calculate the variables of whatever they decide to order. \n\nActually, the book club has been reading some very questionable selections lately anyway. Last month, Brenda—she’s the one who always brings those awful store-bought cookies—she picked this historical fiction novel set in 18th-century France. And the author clearly did absolutely zero research on the agricultural practices of the time. They had a character harvesting tomatoes in early spring! In Paris! It completely ruined the suspension of disbelief for me. I spent half the meeting trying to explain the crop rotation cycles of the era, but they just wanted to talk about the romance plot. Which, frankly, was just as poorly constructed. The pacing was entirely off. \n\nBut yes, I just tell them I have to get back to my spreadsheets. It’s much easier than trying to manage the unpredictability of a restaurant menu.\n\nDoctor: Do you have a strong fear of gaining weight or becoming fat, even though you are currently at a lower weight than your doctor considers healthy?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't call it a fear of becoming fat, exactly. That sounds so... emotional. It's more of a very strong preference for efficiency. A fear of the numbers creeping up and ruining the baseline I've established. If the weight goes up, it means my intake calculations were wrong, and I abhor an error in the data. \n\nSpeaking of data errors, you wouldn't believe the absolute mess I had to untangle at work on Tuesday. One of the junior analysts—I think his name is Kevin, he always wears these terribly scuffed loafers—he had completely misaligned the columns on the Q3 supply chain projection. He had the freight costs merging into the warehousing overhead. I mean, it was a disaster. If I hadn't caught it, they would have presented that report to the regional director. And the regional director is this very severe woman who insists on everything being printed out on physical paper. Who prints spreadsheets in 2023? You have to tape the pages together just to see the quarterly progression. It's incredibly inefficient. I spent three hours reformatting the entire workbook just so it wouldn't look like a patchwork quilt when she printed it. \n\nBut yes, to answer your question... I just don't like it when the scale goes up. It means the system isn't working the way I designed it to.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise, fast, or do anything else to make up for the food you eat or to control your weight?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't really do anything extreme like fasting for days or anything like that. I just stick to the schedule. If I miscalculate, I just adjust the next day's intake. It's just basic mathematics, really. \n\nThough, honestly, speaking of mathematics, I was trying to explain this very simple concept to my neighbor the other day. He was trying to figure out how much fertilizer he needed for his lawn, and he was just guessing! Throwing handfuls of it everywhere. I told him he needed to calculate the square footage and divide by the coverage rate on the bag, but he just looked at me like I was speaking Greek. And his lawn is a complete disaster anyway, it's half crabgrass. I don't understand why people don't just take a systematic approach to these things. It saves so much time and effort in the long run. He also leaves his recycling bin out for three days after pickup, which is a violation of the HOA bylaws, but nobody seems to enforce anything around here anymore. \n\nBut no, I don't do any of those drastic things you mentioned to make up for food. I just keep the numbers balanced.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you have a fear of gaining weight or becoming fat, even though you know you're currently at a lower weight than your doctor considers healthy?\n\nPatient: I know we just touched on this, but... well, yes, if I'm being perfectly honest. If the scale is up even a single pound after a normal dinner or a restaurant meal, I do feel this... this sharp panic. It just feels like I’ve completely lost control of the variables, and I absolutely have to correct it the next day. \n\nRestaurant meals are just impossible to calculate anyway. I actually had to go to my sister's retirement dinner last Thursday at this Italian place downtown. The floor plan was an absolute nightmare. They had the waitstaff crossing directly through the main dining traffic just to get from the kitchen to the patio. It's a massive logistical bottleneck! I watched our waiter—who kept forgetting to refill the water glasses, by the way—collide with a busboy twice. Twice! If management had just implemented a simple circular routing system for the staff, they could increase their table turnover rate by at least fifteen percent. It’s not difficult to figure out. \n\nMy sister didn't even notice the chaos, of course. She was just going on and on about her pension options. Which she hasn't diversified at all, despite my repeated advice. She just leaves it all in a default target-date fund that is far too conservative for the current inflation rate. I offered to build her a forecasting model, but she said she didn't want to look at a spreadsheet at her own party. \n\nBut yes, if I eat in that kind of unpredictable environment and the scale is up the next morning, the panic is quite intense. I just have to fix the numbers immediately.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that your weight or shape has a significant impact on how you see yourself or your sense of worth?\n\nPatient: Well, yes... I suppose it does. I mean, I know I'm an older woman, I'm sixty-three, I'm certainly not trying to look like a twenty-something on a magazine cover. But I still judge how well I'm handling myself, how disciplined I am, by whether my waist and hips look contained in my clothes. If things feel loose and sharp, I know I'm operating efficiently. \n\nSpeaking of clothes, I took a few of my blazers to the dry cleaner on Maple Street last week, and the owner—I think his name is George—has completely reorganized his intake system, and not for the better. He used to have a very logical alphabetical tagging system, but now he's color-coding by the day of the week, which makes absolutely no sense because what happens if someone drops off on a Tuesday but requests a rush order for Wednesday? The colors completely overlap and defeat the purpose of visual sorting. I tried to explain this to him while he was searching for my navy blazer—which took him five minutes, by the way—but he just waved me off. It's so frustrating to watch a perfectly good small business create its own inefficiencies. I almost offered to flowchart a new tagging process for him on the spot. \n\nBut yes, to get back to what you were asking... if my clothes don't fit exactly the way they should, if my shape feels off, I definitely feel like I'm failing at maintaining my own standards.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe your concerns about eating and weight are primarily about maintaining control and discipline in your life, or are they more specifically about a fear of gaining weight or becoming fat?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it's... it's a bit of both, isn't it? I mean, the control is paramount. If I can't control the input and the output, what can I control? But yes, the fear of the weight itself is definitely there. It's the physical manifestation of losing that control. If I see the number go up, or if I feel... heavier, it's terrifying. \n\nIt's like when the city decided to change the traffic light sequencing on 4th Avenue last month. They completely ignored the peak flow data from the morning commute. I sat at my window with a stopwatch for three mornings in a row—just out of curiosity, really—and the idle time at the intersection of 4th and Elm has increased by an average of forty-two seconds per vehicle. Forty-two seconds! Multiply that by the hundreds of cars passing through, and the compounded loss of productivity is staggering. Not to mention the increased emissions. I actually drafted a letter to the city planner with a proposed optimization model using a simple queuing theory approach, but I haven't heard back. Typical municipal inefficiency. They just implement these changes without testing the variables, and suddenly everything is backed up and chaotic. \n\nSo, yes, it's the fear of the chaos, the fear of the weight itself, because the weight means the system has broken down. The discipline is what keeps the fear at bay, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your weight or shape is significantly different from how others might see it, or do you think others misunderstand how much you weigh?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't really know. I haven't exactly taken a poll on how other people perceive my shape. I suppose my sister thinks I'm too thin, but she thinks everyone is too thin unless they're eating a second helping of her heavy cream pasta. \n\nSpeaking of my sister, she finally called me back about the forecasting model I offered to build for her retirement fund. And you know what she said? She said she just trusts the bank's default algorithm. The default algorithm! I tried to explain to her that those algorithms are built on generalized actuarial tables that don't account for individual risk tolerance or localized inflation spikes. It's just lazy programming. I spent three hours over the weekend pulling historical data on target-date funds, just to show her the standard deviation in their returns during market downturns. The variance is honestly shocking if you actually look at the raw numbers. I even put it into a very clean, easy-to-read scatter plot for her, but she told me I was overcomplicating things. Overcomplicating! I'm trying to save her from a ten percent reduction in her purchasing power over the next decade! \n\nBut anyway, I don't know if people misunderstand my weight. I just know what the numbers on my own scale say, and that's the only data point I really trust.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your overall sense of self-worth depends heavily on your ability to control your weight or shape?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, it really does. I think I touched on this earlier, but I know I’m an older woman now. I’m not trying to look young or pretend I'm in my twenties. But I absolutely still judge how well I’m handling myself—my whole sense of value, really—by whether my waist and hips look properly contained in my clothes. If they are, I know I'm doing a good job. \n\nSpeaking of handling things properly, I actually had to go down to the post office yesterday to mail that scatter plot to my sister—since she refuses to open attachments on her tablet—and the queue management there is just an absolute disaster. They had three windows open, using a single-queue system. Now, theoretically, a single-queue system should minimize average wait times, but they had one teller processing passport applications! Passports take at least fifteen minutes. So the entire line kept stalling because their routing method didn't account for transaction-type variability. I stood there for twenty-five minutes just to mail one standard envelope. I actually tried to explain to the floor manager that they need a dedicated express line for transactions under two minutes—just basic triage—but he looked at me like I was speaking Greek. It’s staggering how people just accept such broken systems without trying to optimize them. \n\nBut, um... what was the question? Oh, right, my self-worth. Yes, if my clothes don't feel right on my hips, I feel like I'm failing. It's just how I measure my own discipline.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I almost offered to flowchart a new tagging process for him on the spot. \n\nBut yes, to get back to what you were asking... if my clothes don't fit exactly the way they should, if my shape feels off, I definitely feel like I'm failing at maintaining my own standards.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe your concerns about eating and weight are primarily about maintaining control and discipline in your life, or are they more specifically about a fear of gaining weight or becoming fat?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it's... it's a bit of both, isn't it? I mean, the control is paramount. If I can't control the input and the output, what can I control? But yes, the fear of the weight itself is definitely there. It's the physical manifestation of losing that control. If I see the number go up, or if I feel... heavier, it's terrifying. \n\nIt's like when the city decided to change the traffic light sequencing on 4th Avenue last month. They completely ignored the peak flow data from the morning commute. I sat at my window with a stopwatch for three mornings in a row—just out of curiosity, really—and the idle time at the intersection of 4th and Elm has increased by an average of forty-two seconds per vehicle. Forty-two seconds! Multiply that by the hundreds of cars passing through, and the compounded loss of productivity is staggering. Not to mention the increased emissions. I actually drafted a letter to the city planner with a proposed optimization model using a simple queuing theory approach, but I haven't heard back. Typical municipal inefficiency. They just implement these changes without testing the variables, and suddenly everything is backed up and chaotic. \n\nSo, yes, it's the fear of the chaos, the fear of the weight itself, because the weight means the system has broken down. The discipline is what keeps the fear at bay, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your weight or shape is significantly different from how others might see it, or do you think others misunderstand how much you weigh?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't really know. I tried to explain to her that those algorithms are built on generalized actuarial tables that don't account for individual risk tolerance or localized inflation spikes. It's just lazy programming. I spent three hours over the weekend pulling historical data on target-date funds, just to show her the standard deviation in their returns during market downturns. The variance is honestly shocking if you actually look at the raw numbers. I even put it into a very clean, easy-to-read scatter plot for her, but she told me I was overcomplicating things. Overcomplicating! I'm trying to save her from a ten percent reduction in her purchasing power over the next decade! \n\nBut anyway, I don't know if people misunderstand my weight. I just know what the numbers on my own scale say, and that's the only data point I really trust.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your overall sense of self-worth depends heavily on your ability to control your weight or shape?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, it really does. I think I touched on this earlier, but I know I’m an older woman now. I’m not trying to look young or pretend I'm in my twenties. Passports take at least fifteen minutes. So the entire line kept stalling because their routing method didn't account for transaction-type variability. Well, it's... it's definitely about the weight itself, though the two are so intertwined, aren't they? If the scale is up even a pound—just one pound—after a normal dinner, or God forbid, a restaurant meal, I feel this sharp, sudden panic. It’s a very specific fear that I’ve gained weight, and it immediately translates into feeling like I’ve completely lost control. I just know I have to correct it the very next day. 

Restaurant meals are the absolute worst for that, actually. The variability in portion sizes and hidden sodium is a nightmare for data tracking. I was at this little Italian place on 8th Street last Thursday—just a small retirement dinner for a colleague from the firm—and the way they handled the seating arrangement was incredibly inefficient. They had pushed three rectangular tables together, but they didn't account for the structural pillars in the room, so the waitstaff had this massive bottleneck every time they tried to serve the middle section. I watched a busboy have to reroute his path five times just to clear the salad plates. It was agonizing to watch. I actually sketched out a more optimal floor plan on my napkin using a basic grid system to maximize flow efficiency. If they just angled the tables forty-five degrees, they could increase their table turnover rate by at least fifteen percent. I left the napkin under my water glass, hoping the manager might find it. 

But yes, the panic is real. If the number goes up, it means I'm getting heavier, and that means I have to fix it immediately.

I couldn't hear a word she was saying across the table. I had to use the flashlight on my phone just to read the vinaigrette options. One of the junior analysts—I think his name is Kevin, he always wears these terribly scuffed loafers—he had completely misaligned the columns on the Q3 supply chain projection. He had the freight costs merging into the warehousing overhead. I mean, it was a disaster. If I hadn't caught it, they would have presented that report to the regional director. And the regional director is this very severe woman who insists on everything being printed out on physical paper. Who prints spreadsheets in 2023? You have to tape the pages together just to see the quarterly progression. It's incredibly inefficient. I just don't like it when the scale goes up.
